Proof Theory for Bimodal Provability Logics
Borja Sierra Miranda, Thomas Studer

Abstract
We provide the first (non-labelled) sequent calculi for bimodal provability logics with ’usual’ provability predicates. In particular, we introduce calculi for the logics 𝖢𝖲 \mathsf{CS} , 𝖢𝖲𝖬 \mathsf{CSM} and 𝖤𝖱 \mathsf{ER} . Additionally, we present non-wellfounded versions of our calculi, and use them to establish a cut-elimination procedure. Finally, we prove the first interpolation results for these logics showing that they all enjoy the uniform Lyndon interpolation property.



1
Introduction

Provability logic is the area of modal logic that studies the notion of proof through the lense of modal logic, by interpreting the modal operators as
provable in some mathematical theory
(usually arithmetical theories).
One of the most important results in the area is Solovay’s characterization
[
41
]
of the provability logic of
\mathsf{PA}
(Peano Arithmetic) with respect to
\mathsf{PA}
, being the logic
\mathsf{GL}
, and with respect to the standard model
\mathbb{N}
, being the logic
\mathsf{GLS}
.

A natural generalization is to consider a modal language with two modalities,
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}
and
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}
, which will lead to bimodal provability logics.
The modality
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}
is interpreted as provability in some theory
T
while
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}
is interpreted as provability in another theory
S
.
The bimodal language is thus capable of expressing relationships between provability in
T
and provability in
S
.
For instance,
S
is an extension of
T
is expressed by the schema
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\phi
called monotonicity, the statement that the theory
S
proves every instance of soundness in
T
is expressed by the schema
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\to\phi)
, and that
S
decides provability in
T
is expressed by
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\vee\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\neg\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi
.

In this paper, we will develop the proof theory of three major bimodal provability logics:
\mathsf{CS}
,
\mathsf{CSM}
, and
\mathsf{ER}
.
The logic
\mathsf{CS}
is the minimal bimodal provability logic
[
40
]
(see also the discussion in
[
7
]
). The logic
\mathsf{CSM}
is the extension of
\mathsf{CS}
with the monotoncity schema
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\phi
mentioned before.
The logic
\mathsf{ER}
, discoverd by Carlson
[
14
]
and indepently by Montagna
[
30
]
, is the provability logic of
sound theories
T
and
S
where
S
contains the local reflection principle for
T
. Thus
\mathsf{ER}
is the extension of
\mathsf{CS}
with the principle of essential reflexivity
\Box_{1}(\Box_{0}\phi\to\phi)
.
Note that in
\mathsf{CSM}
and
\mathsf{ER}
, we have that the theory
S
is an extension of the theory
T
.
The provability logic of extensions is one of the main topics in bimodal provability logic
[
7
,
8
]
.

The essential technical tool for our proof-theoretic study of
\mathsf{CS}
,
\mathsf{CSM}
, and
\mathsf{ER}
are non-wellfounded proofs.
Proof systems of this kind have succesfully been developed to a plethora of logics with explicit fixpoints, see, e.g.,
[
12
,
3
,
2
,
13
,
25
,
4
,
16
,
38
]
among many others.
Shamkanov
[
35
]
has observed that there is also a close connection between non-wellfounded proofs and the provability logic
\mathsf{GL}
, although the fixpoints of this logic are not explicit. Later, he and Savateev have extended this approach to capture the logics
\mathsf{Grz}

[
32
]
and
\mathsf{wGrz}

[
33
]
.
Despite these results, almost no proof-theoretic work has been carried out for bimodal provability logics with two ‘usual’
[
23
]
provability predicates.
1
1

1

There are also bi- and polymodal logics, e.g.,
\mathsf{GLB}
and
\mathsf{GLP}
, modeling stronger notions of provability like
\omega
-provability. For some of these logics, there are sequent systems available, e.g.,
[
36
]
. Note, however, that these polymodal logics are not extensions of
\mathsf{CS}
, but in some sense orthogonal to it.
The only exception is
[
5
]
, which introduces a non-wellfounded labelled sequent calculus for
\mathsf{CS}
.

One important application of non-wellfounded proofs is to establish interpolation properties, see, e.g.,
[
1
,
10
,
24
,
22
,
39
]
. We, too, will use our systems to show interpolation results for
\mathsf{CS}
,
\mathsf{CSM}
, and
\mathsf{ER}
.
Our main contributions are as follows:

1.

We provide the first sequent calculi for ’usual’ bi-modal provability logics. Note than in the case of
\mathsf{CS}
, there is a labelled calculus available. Our approach, however, does not require the use of labels and is thus syntactically pure. Moreover, it does not only work for
\mathsf{CS}
, but can be generalized to
\mathsf{CSM}
and
\mathsf{ER}
.

2.

We provide syntactic cut-elimination results for our calculi. Hence we obtain completeness of the cut-free systems.

3.

We provide the first interpolation results for
\mathsf{CS}
,
\mathsf{CSM}
, and
\mathsf{ER}
. Actually, we directly prove uniform Lyndon interpolation (see
[
26
]
) for these three logics, which is the strongest form of interpolation.

4.

Last but not least, we make the technical observation that the proof theory of
\mathsf{ER}
is rather simple compared to its semantics.

Let us elaborate on the last point.

\mathsf{ER}
is not Kripke complete, and we have to enrich Kripke models with a topology (thus working in the general Kripke semantics setting) to obtain a completeness result for
\mathsf{ER}

[
45
]
.
On the proof-theoretic side, the picture is simpler: we only have to work with two types of sequents.
Sequents of the shape
\Gamma\Rightarrow_{0}\Delta
will be interpreted as usual, i.e.,
\bigwedge\Gamma\to\bigvee\Delta
, while sequents of the shape
\Gamma\Rightarrow_{1}\Delta
will be interpreted as
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}(\bigwedge\Gamma\to\bigvee\Delta)
.
This kind of methodology has been previously seen in sequent calculi for the provability logic
\mathsf{GLS}

[
9
,
15
,
27
]
(the provability logic of Peano arithmetic according to true arithmetic).
However, in the systems for
\mathsf{GLS}
, it is impossible to go from
\Rightarrow_{1}
-sequents to
\Rightarrow_{0}
-sequents, while in our system, both direction (from
\Rightarrow_{1}
to
\Rightarrow_{0}
and vice versa) are possible.

Structure of the paper.

In Section
2
, we will introduce the logics and concepts needed for the rest of the paper, in particular the bimodal logics
\mathsf{CS}
,
\mathsf{CSM}
, and
\mathsf{ER}
, (modal) equational systems, uniform Lyndon interpolation, and the basics of non-wellfounded (local progress) proof theory.
In Section
3
, we will introduce wellfounded (finite) sequent calculi for
\mathsf{CS}
,
\mathsf{CSM}
and
\mathsf{ER}
. We will establish the correspondence of these sequent calculi with cut and the Hilbert-style axiomatizations of the logics.
In Section
4
, we will introduce non-wellfounded sequent calculi for
\mathsf{CS}
,
\mathsf{CSM}
and
\mathsf{ER}
, establish translations with the wellfounded calculi, and prove cut elimination for all of them.
Finally, in Section
5
, we will prove uniform Lyndon interpolation for
\mathsf{CS}
and
\mathsf{ER}
via interpolation templates and for
\mathsf{CSM}
via an adequate interpretation of
\mathsf{CSM}
in
\mathsf{CS}
.

2
Preliminaries

In this section we will introduce the concepts from modal logic and proof theory needed for the paper.

2.1
Bimodal provabiliy logic

We fix an infinite countable set
\mathrm{Var}
whose elements will be called
propositional variables
.

{definition}

We define the
language of bimodal logic

\mathcal{L}_{\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\leq 1}}
by the the following grammar

\phi::=p\mid\bot\mid\phi\to\phi\mid\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\mid\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\phi,

where
p\in\mathrm{Var}
.
The expressions of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\leq 1}}
are called (bimodal) formulas.

As usual, we define the following abreviations:

\displaystyle\neg\phi:=\phi\to\bot,

\displaystyle\phi\vee\psi:=\neg\phi\to\psi,

\displaystyle\phi\wedge\psi:=\neg(\neg\phi\vee\neg\psi),

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{\scalebox{1.1}{$\meddiamond$}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi:=\neg\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\neg\phi.

Given a formula
\phi
we define its complexity, denoted
|\phi|
as

\displaystyle|p|=|\bot|=1,

\displaystyle|\phi_{0}\to\phi_{1}|=|\phi_{0}|+|\phi_{1}|+1,

\displaystyle|\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi_{0}|=|\phi_{0}|+1.

If
\phi,\psi_{0},\ldots,\psi_{n-1}
are formulas and
p_{0},\ldots,p_{n-1}\in\mathrm{Var}
, then
\phi[\psi_{0}/p_{0},\ldots,\psi_{n-1}/p_{n-1}]
denotes the result of simultenously substituting every occurrence of
p_{i}
in
\phi
by
\psi_{i}
for
i<n
.
The formulas of shape
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi
will be called
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits
-formulas.

A
(bimodal) normal logic

L
is a set of formulas with the following properties:

1.

Every classical propositional tautology in the language
\mathcal{L}_{\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\leq 1}}
is in
L
.

2.

(Axiom
\mathrm{K}_{i}
) For any
\phi,\psi
,
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\phi\to\psi)\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\psi
is in
L
for
i\in\left\{0,1\right\}
.

3.

L
is closed under the rules

\phi

\phi\to\psi

\mathrm{MP}

\psi

\phi

\mathrm{Nec}_{0}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi

\phi

\mathrm{Nec}_{1}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\phi

The elements of
L
are called the
theorems of
L
, and we will usually denote
\phi\in L
as
L\vdash\phi
.
For a set of formulas
\Gamma
, we will denote
L\vdash\Gamma
to mean
L\vdash\phi
for any
\phi\in\Gamma
.

Given a bimodal logic
L
we define its
normal extension by a set of formulas
\Gamma
, denoted
L\oplus\Gamma
, as the smallest bimodal logic containing
\Gamma
.
We also define the smallest bimodal normal logic as the intersection of all bimodal normal logics and we denote it as
\mathsf{K}_{2}
, i.e., is the smallest set of formulas containing the propositonal tautologies, axiom
(\mathrm{K}_{i})
for
i\leq 1
and is closed under
(\mathrm{MP})
and
(\mathrm{Nec}_{i})
for
i\leq 1
.

We proceed to define the bimodal logics
\mathsf{CS}
,
\mathsf{CSM}
and
\mathsf{ER}
.

{definition}

Define the following sets of formulas:

•

\mathrm{L}_{i}=\left\{\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\phi)\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\mid\phi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\leq 1}}\right\}
,

•

\mathrm{M}_{i,j}=\left\{\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{j}\phi\mid\phi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\leq 1}}\right\}
,

•

\mathrm{C}_{i,j}=\left\{\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{j}\phi\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{j}\phi\mid\phi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\leq 1}}\right\}
,

•

\mathrm{ER}_{i,j}=\left\{\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{j}\phi\to\phi)\mid\phi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\leq 1}}\right\}
.

Then we define the following bimodal logics:

•

\mathsf{CS}=\mathsf{K}_{2}\oplus(\mathrm{L}_{0}\cup\mathrm{L}_{1}\cup\mathrm{C}_{0,1}\cup\mathrm{C}_{1,0})
,

•

\mathsf{ER}=\mathsf{CS}\oplus\mathrm{ER}_{1,0}=\mathsf{CSM}\oplus\mathrm{ER}_{1,0}
.

•

\mathsf{CSM}=\mathsf{CS}\oplus\mathrm{M}_{0,1}
,

Note that
\mathsf{CS}\oplus\mathrm{ER}_{1,0}\vdash\mathrm{M}_{0,1}
.
We define the set of formulas
4_{i}=\left\{\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\mid\phi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\leq 1}}\right\}
, i.e.,
\mathrm{C}_{i,i}
.
Then, by the same reasoning that can be carried in
\mathsf{GL}
, we obtain the following result.

{lemma}

\mathsf{CS}\vdash 4_{i}
,
\mathsf{CSM}\vdash 4_{i}
and
\mathsf{ER}\vdash 4_{i}
for
i\leq 1
.

2.2
Interpolation and modal equation systems

The main application of our non-wellfounded calculi will be to establish uniform Lyndon interpolation.
In this subsection we remember the related concepts that will be necessary for the proof.
A
vocabulary
is just a set of propositonal variables.
First we define the positive and negative vocabulary of a formula.

{definition}

Given a formula
\phi
we define its
positive and negative vocabulary
, denoted by
\mathrm{Voc}_{+}(\phi)
and
\mathrm{Voc}_{-}(\phi)
, respectively, by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Voc}_{+}(p)=\left\{p\right\},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Voc}_{-}(p)=\varnothing,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Voc}_{+}(\bot)=\varnothing,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Voc}_{-}(\bot)=\varnothing,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Voc}_{+}(\phi_{0}\to\phi_{1})=\mathrm{Voc}_{-}(\phi_{0})\cup\mathrm{Voc}_{+}(\phi_{1}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Voc}_{-}(\phi_{0}\to\phi_{1})=\mathrm{Voc}_{+}(\phi_{0})\cup\mathrm{Voc}_{-}(\phi_{1}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Voc}_{+}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi_{0})=\mathrm{Voc}_{+}(\phi),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Voc}_{-}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi_{0})=\mathrm{Voc}_{-}(\phi).

We will write
\overline{+}
to mean
-
and
\overline{-}
to mean
+
.

The following lemma can be proved by induction on the complexity of
\phi
.

{lemma}

Let
p
be a propositional variable and
\phi(p_{0},\ldots,p_{n-1},q_{0},\ldots,q_{m-1})
,
\psi_{0},\ldots,\psi_{n-1}
, and
\chi_{0},\ldots,\chi_{m-1}
be formulas.
If
\mathrm{Voc}_{-}(\phi)\cap\left\{p_{0},\ldots,p_{n-1}\right\}=\varnothing
and
\mathrm{Voc}_{+}(\phi)\cap\left\{q_{0},\ldots,q_{m-1}\right\}=\varnothing
, then for
b\in\left\{+,-\right\}

\mathrm{Voc}_{b}(\phi(\psi_{0},\ldots,\psi_{n-1},\chi_{0},\ldots,\chi_{m-1}))\subseteq\\
\mathrm{Voc}_{b}(\phi)\setminus\left\{p_{0},\ldots,p_{n-1},q_{0},\ldots,q_{m-1}\right\}\cup\bigcup_{i<n}\mathrm{Voc}_{b}(\psi_{i})\cup\bigcup_{j<m}\mathrm{Voc}_{\overline{b}}(\chi_{j}).

Uniform Lyndon interpolation says that given a formula
\phi
and vocabularies
V_{+}
and
V_{-}
there is a formula which best approximates
\phi
to the right in the set of formulas whose positive vocabulary is contained in
V_{+}
and whose negative vocabulary is contained in
V_{-}
.
The exact definition is below.

{definition}

A logic
L
has
uniform Lyndon interpolation
if for any formula
\phi
and vocabularies
V_{+},V_{-}
there is a formula
\iota
, called
interpolant
of
\phi
for
(V_{+},V_{-})
in
L
, such that

1.

\mathrm{Voc}_{+}(\iota)\subseteq V_{+}
and
\mathrm{Voc}_{-}(\iota)\subseteq V_{-}
,

2.

L\vdash\phi\to\iota
, and

3.

for any
\psi
such that
\mathrm{Voc}_{+}(\psi)\subseteq V_{+}
and
\mathrm{Voc}_{-}(\psi)\subseteq V_{-}
,
L\vdash\phi\to\psi
implies
L\vdash\iota\to\psi
.

We notice that given two interpolants
\iota_{0},\iota_{1}
of
\phi
for
(V_{+},V_{-})
in
L
we have that
L\vdash\iota_{0}\leftrightarrow\iota_{1}
.
In words, interpolants are unique up to logical equivalence.
For this reason it is common to talk about
the
interpolant of a formula, even if the uniqueness only holds up to logical equivalence.

For the calculation of interpolants, it will be necessary to use a generalization of the notion of fixpoint called equational systems.
These equational systems are commonly used for
\mu
-calculus (e.g. see
[
18
]
), while in provability logic the concept is usually called simultaneous fixed-point theorem (e.g see
[
17
]
, called
n
-ary fixed point theorem).
2
2

2

In fact, the idea that solving individual fixpoints suffices to solve simultaneous fixpoints is proven in full generality in Bekić Theorem, see
[
6
]
.

We will adapt the concept to our needs, as the Lyndon property forces us to keep track of the polarity of variables.
We remember that a formula
\phi
is modalized in a variable
p
if all the occurences of
p
in
\phi
are under the scope of a
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}
.

{definition}

[Lyndon equational systems]
Let
V_{+},V_{-}
be vocabularies and
\bar{p}=(p_{0},\ldots,p_{n-1})
be a finite sequence of pairwise different variables not occuring in
V_{+}\cup V_{-}
.
A
Lyndon equational system over
(\bar{p},V_{+},V_{-})
is a set of triples
\mathcal{E}=\left\{(p_{i},b_{i},\phi_{i})\mid i<n\right\}
such that
b_{i}\in\left\{+,-\right\}
,
\phi_{i}
is a formula,
\mathrm{Voc}_{b_{i}}(\phi_{i})\subseteq V_{+}\cup B_{+}
and
\mathrm{Voc}_{\overline{b_{i}}}(\phi_{i})\subseteq V_{-}\cup B_{-}
, where
B_{+}=\left\{p_{j}\mid j<n,b_{j}=+\right\}
and
B_{-}=\left\{p_{j}\mid j<n,b_{j}=-\right\}
.
The elements of
\bar{p}
are called
unknowns
and those of
\mathcal{E}
are called
equations
.

A
solution in
L
to
\mathcal{E}
is a sequence
(\psi_{0},\ldots,\psi_{n-1})
such that for each
i\in n
, we have

\mathrm{Voc}_{b_{i}}(\psi_{i})\subseteq V_{+}
,
\mathrm{Voc}_{\overline{b_{i}}}(\psi_{i})\subseteq V_{-}
and
L\vdash\psi_{i}\leftrightarrow\phi_{i}[\psi_{0}/p_{0},\ldots,\psi_{n-1}/p_{n-1}]
.

\mathcal{E}
is said to be

1.

Solvable in
L
if it has a solution in
L
.

2.

Simple
if
\phi_{i}
is a
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits
-formula for
i<n
.

3.

Modalized
if
\phi_{i}
is modalized in
p_{0},\ldots,p_{i}
.

4.

Positive
if
b_{i}={+}
for
i<n
.

Given an equational system
\mathcal{E}
over
(\bar{p},V_{+},V_{-})
with solution
(\psi_{0},\ldots,\psi_{n-1})
we will also call the substitution
(\cdot)^{*}
where
p_{i}^{*}=\psi_{i}
and
q^{*}=q
for
q
not in
\bar{p}
a solution of
\mathcal{E}
.

The fundamental tool to solve Lyndon equational system is the concept of Lyndon fixpoint.

{definition}

[Lyndon Fixpoints]
Let
\phi(p)
and
\psi
be formulas.
We say that
\psi
is a
Lyndon fixpoint of
\phi
(with respect to
p
) in
L
if
\mathrm{Voc}_{b}(\psi)\subseteq\mathrm{Voc}_{b}(\phi)\setminus\left\{p\right\}
for
b\in\left\{+,-\right\}
and
L\vdash\psi\leftrightarrow\phi(\psi)
.
A logic
L
is said to have
basic Lyndon fixpoints
if every
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits
-formula has a Lyndon fixpoint with respect to any variable, and is said to have
positive modalized Lyndon fixpoints
if every formula
\phi
has a Lyndon fixpoint with respect to the variables in the set
\left\{p\in\mathrm{Var}\setminus\mathrm{Voc}_{-}(\phi)\mid\phi\text{ modalized in }p\right\}
.

If
L
is a bimodal normal logic with basic Lyndon fixpoints then it is possible to show that
L
has positive modalized Lyndon fixpoints and every positive modalized equational systems have solution in
L
(see Appendix
A
; the unimodal case, which is completely analogous, was shown at
[
39
]
).
We proceed to show that
\mathsf{CS}
,
\mathsf{CSM}
and
\mathsf{ER}
have basic Lyndon fixpoints.
The proof follows
[
40
, pg. 78]
, where it is proven that
\mathsf{GL}
has basic fixpoints, although it needs some tiny adaptation for the bimodal case (in particular the use of
\mathrm{C}_{1,0}
and
\mathrm{C}_{0,1}
).

{lemma}

[Substitution theorem]
For any formulas
\phi(p),\psi,\chi
we have that

\mathsf{CS}\vdash(\psi\leftrightarrow\chi)\wedge\bigwedge_{i\leq 1}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\psi\leftrightarrow\chi)\to(\phi(\psi)\leftrightarrow\phi(\chi)).

Proof.

By induction on the complexity of
\phi
.
∎

{lemma}

\mathsf{CS}
has basic Lyndon fixpoints, in particular
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(p)
has
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top)
as a fixpoint.
As a corollary
\mathsf{CSM}
and
\mathsf{ER}
also have basic Lyndon fixpoints.

Proof.

We have the following reasoning

\displaystyle\mathsf{CS}\vdash\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top)\to(\top\leftrightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top))

\displaystyle\text{propositional reasoning},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CS}\vdash\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top)\to(\top\leftrightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top))\wedge\bigwedge_{i\leq 1}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\top\leftrightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top))

\displaystyle\text{by $(\mathrm{C}_{\overline{i},i})$ and $(4_{i})$},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CS}\vdash\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top)\to(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top)\leftrightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top)))

\displaystyle\text{by Substitution Lemma}.

Then it is clear that
\mathsf{CS}\vdash\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top)\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top))
, for the inverse implication we have the following reasoning.

\displaystyle\mathsf{CS}\vdash\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top)\to(\top\leftrightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top))\wedge\bigwedge_{i\leq 1}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\top\leftrightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top))

\displaystyle\text{shown above},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CS}\vdash\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top)\to(\phi(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top))\leftrightarrow\phi(\top))

\displaystyle\text{by Substitution Lemma},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CS}\vdash\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top)\to(\phi(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top))\to\phi(\top))

\displaystyle\text{by propositional reasoning},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CS}\vdash\phi(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top))\to(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top)\to\phi(\top))

\displaystyle\text{by propositional reasoning},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CS}\vdash\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top))\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top)\to\phi(\top))

\displaystyle\text{by $\mathrm{Nec}_{i}$ and $(\mathrm{K}_{i})$},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CS}\vdash\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top))\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi(\top)

\displaystyle\text{by $(\mathrm{L}_{i})$}.

∎

We obtain the desired properties of
\mathsf{CS}
,
\mathsf{CSM}
and
\mathsf{ER}
.

2.1
.

We have the following.

1.

\mathsf{CS}
,
\mathsf{CSM}
and
\mathsf{ER}
have positive modalized Lyndon fixpoints.

2.

Any positive modalized Lyndon equational system has a solution in
\mathsf{CS}
,
\mathsf{CSM}
and
\mathsf{ER}
.

2.3
Local progress proof theory

Let us start by fixing the notions concerning trees.
Given a set
X
we will write
X^{*}
(
X^{+}
) to mean the set of (non-empty) finite sequences of elements in
X
,
\epsilon
will denote the empty sequence.
As usual, we will write
w\leq v
to mean that
w
is an initial prefix of
v
and
(w,v]
to mean the set
\left\{u\in X^{*}\mid w<u\leq v\right\}
.
A (finitely branching) tree on
A
is a function
T
whose image is contained in
A
and whose domain is a prefix-closed non-empty subset of
\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that for every
w\in\domain(T)
there is an unique natural number
k
(called the
arity of
w
in
T
) such that
wi\in\domain(T)
iff
i<k
.
The
wi\in\domain(T)
are also called the
immediate successors of
w
.
Note that the domain of any tree always contains the word
\epsilon
, and its called the
root of
T
.

The elements of
\domain(T)
are also called the
nodes of
T
, the
0
-ary nodes are called
leaves
and the rest of the nodes are called
interior nodes
.
Finally, an
infinite branch in a tree
T
is sequence of nodes
(w_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
w_{0}=\epsilon
and for each
i
there is a
j
such that
w_{i+1}=w_{i}j
.

We fix a set
Seq
whose elements we will call
sequents
, a sequent rule is a subset of
\text{Seq}^{+}
.
Given a rule
R
, we say that
(S_{0},\ldots,S_{n-1},S)
is an
instance of
R
with premises
S_{0},\ldots,S_{n-1}
and conclusion
S
if
(S_{0},\ldots,S_{n-1},S)\in R
.
A rule
R
is said to be
n
-ary
if
R\subseteq\text{Seq}^{n+1}
.
We introduce the kind of sequent calculi we are going to use, called
local progress sequent calculi

[
37
]
.

{definition}

A
local progress sequent calculus
is a pair
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{R},(L_{R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}})
where
\mathcal{R}
is a set of sequent rules and each
L_{R}
is a function that takes an instance
r=(S_{0},\ldots,S_{n-1},S)
of
R
and returns a subset of
\left\{0,\ldots,n-1\right\}
.

\mathcal{G}
is said to be
wellfounded
if each
L_{R}
is the constant function returning
\varnothing
.

We are prepared to define the notion of proof in a local progress calculus.
From the defintion of proof we can infer that a wellfounded sequent calculus is just a sequent calculus in the usual (wellfounded) proof theory.

{definition}

Given a local progress sequent calculus
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{R},(L_{R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}})
, a
preproof in
\mathcal{G}
is a tree with labels in
\text{Seq}\times\mathcal{R}
such that for each node
w
with immediate successors
w0,\ldots,w(n-1)
we have that
(S_{0},\ldots,S_{n-1},S)\in R
where
S
is the sequent at
w
,
R
is the rule at
w
and
S_{i}
is the sequent at
wi
.

Let
w
be a node in
\pi
with immediate successors
w0,\ldots,w(n-1)
.
We say that
wi
is a
progressing node
if
i\in L_{R}(r)
where
R
is the rule at
w
and
r=(S_{0},\ldots,S_{n-1},S)
where
S
is the sequent at
w
and
S_{i}
the sequent at
wi
.
A proof is a preproof in which any infinite branch has infinitely many progressing nodes.

We will write
\mathcal{G}\vdash S
to mean that
S
is provable in
\mathcal{G}
and
\pi\vdash_{\mathcal{G}}S
to mean that
\pi
is a proof of
S
in
\mathcal{G}
, omitting the subscript
G
when it is clear from context.
It will be common to write an instance
(S_{0},\ldots,S_{n-1},S)
of a rule
R
as

S_{0}

\cdots

S_{n-1}

R

S

Given a proof
\pi
in
\mathcal{G}
we will define its main local fragment as the finite tree obtained from cutting the tree at the first progressing nodes from the root (in particular removing the progressing nodes).
Figure
1
shows a picture of how a proof in a local progress calculus looks like.
The gray triangle at the bottom is the main local fragment, while the circular nodes are non-progressing nodes and the square nodes progressing nodes.
The
local height of
\pi
, denoted
\mathrm{lhg}(\pi)
, is the height of its main local fragment.
The
local rules of
\pi
, denoted
\mathrm{lRul}(\pi)
, is the set of rules occuring in the main local fragment.
We will say that
\pi
is
locally
R
-free
if
R\not\in\mathrm{lRul}(\pi)
.
We note that if
\mathcal{G}
is a wellfounded calculus the notion of local height agrees with the usual notion of height and the local rules is the set of rules occuring in the proof.

\vdots

Figure 1:
Structure of proofs in local progress calculi

Given a local progress calculus
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{R},(L_{R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}})
and a rule
R^{\prime}
we define
\mathcal{G}+R^{\prime}
as the local progress calculus with rules
\mathcal{R}\cup\left\{R^{\prime}\right\}
and
L_{R^{\prime}}
the constant function returning
\varnothing
.
Rules can interact with local progress calculi in different ways, the following definition introduce some of these ways and the theorem below shows that some of them are equivalent.

{definition}

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a sequent calculus and
R
be a rule.
We say that
R
is

1.

R
admissible if for any instance
(S_{0},\ldots,S_{n-1},S)\in R
we have that
\pi_{i}\vdash_{\mathcal{G}}S_{i}
for
i<n
implies the existence of
\pi\vdash_{\mathcal{G}}S
.
In addition we say that
R
is
admissible preserving local height
if
\mathrm{lhg}(\pi)\leq\max_{i<n}\mathrm{lhg}(\pi_{i})
and

admissible preserving local rules
if
\mathrm{lRul}(\pi)\subseteq\bigcup_{i<n}\mathrm{lRul}(\pi_{i})
.

2.

R
eliminable if
\mathcal{G}+R\vdash S
implies
\mathcal{G}\vdash S
.

3.

R
locally admissible if for any instance
(S_{0},\ldots,S_{n-1},S)\in R
we have that if there are locally
R
-free
\pi_{i}\vdash_{\mathcal{G}}S_{i}
for
i<n
then there is a locally
R
-free
\pi\vdash_{\mathcal{G}}S
.

4.

An
n
-ary rule
R
is
i
-invertible in
\mathcal{G}
(where
i<n
) if the rule

\left\{(S,S_{i})\mid(S_{0},\ldots,S_{n-1},S)\in R\right\}

is admissible.

R
is
invertible
if it is
i
-invertible for each
i<n
.

We will talk about invertibility preserving local height and/or local rules with the obvious meaning.

Given an admissible rule
R
in
\mathcal{G}
,an instance
(S_{0},\ldots,S_{n-1},S)\in R
and proofs
\pi_{i}\vdash_{\mathcal{G}}S_{i}
for
i<n
, we will write
R(\pi_{0},\ldots,\pi_{n-1})
to mean a proof of
S
in
\mathcal{G}
that exists by admissibility.
For the invertibility of a rule
R
we will write instead
\mathrm{Inv}_{R}(\pi)
.

{remark}

Let us briefly discuss our choice of terminology.
Lorenzen
[
29
]
introduced admissible rules as those rules that can be added to a calculus without changing the set of provable formulas.
Shortly thereafter, Schütte
[
34
]
gave the definition of admissiblity that also we use in Definition
2.3
.
Nowadays, admissibility is often used with respect to a logic and not a calculus, see, e.g.,
[
31
]
. Then a rule
R
is said to be admissible in a logic
L
if it holds that if all premises of
R
are elements of
L
, then so is its conclusion.

Over traditional well-foundend calculi, Schütte’s definition of a rule
R
being admissible in a calculus
\mathcal{G}
is equivalent to saying that
R
is eliminable in
\mathcal{G}
(in the sense of Definition
2.3
). This is no longer the case for non-wellfounded calculi. In that context, the appropriate notion is local admissibility as shown in the next theorem.

Gentzen
[
19
]
originally stated his Hauptsatz as
each derivation can be transformed into a derivation of the same endsequent that does not use cut.

Although he mentions a transformation, there are also many non-constructive cut-elimination results available in the literature, see, e.g.,
[
42
,
43
]
for early examples.
Also our definition of eliminability does not require an effective procedure.
In case we want to talk about the effectiveness of our methodology, we will say effective cut elimination explicitely.

The following is the key result of local progress proof theory (for details see
[
21
]
).

2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a local progress sequent calculus, then
R
is eliminable in
\mathcal{G}
iff
R
is locally admissible in
\mathcal{G}
.
If
\mathcal{G}
is wellfounded, then both are equivalent to
R
is admissible in
\mathcal{G}
.

Finally, sometimes we will need to work with non-wellfounded proofs that have a particular finite representation.
To define this notion we introduce the notion of trees with backedges.
A
tree with backedges
is an ordered pair
\tau=(T,(\cdot)^{\circ})
such that
T
is a finite tree and
(\cdot)^{\circ}
is a function from a subset of the leafs of
\tau
to the nodes of
\tau
such that if
w
is in the domain then
w^{\circ}<w
, the nodes in the domain of
(\cdot)^{\circ}
are called
repeat nodes
.

{definition}

Given a local progress calculus
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{R},(L_{R})_{R\in\mathcal{R}})
, a
cyclic preproof in
\mathcal{G}
is a tree with backedges
\pi=(T,(\cdot)^{\circ})
on
\text{Seq}\times\mathcal{R}
such that for any non-repeat node
w
with immediate successors
w0
, …,
w(n-1)
, we have that
(S_{0},\ldots,S_{n-1},S)\in R
, where
S
is the sequent at
w
,
R
is the rule at
w
and
S_{i}
is the sequent at
wi
,
and for any repeat node
w
,
w
and
w^{\circ}
have the same sequent and rule.
A
cyclic proof in
\mathcal{G}
is a cyclic preproof such that for any repeat node
w
there is a progressing node in
(w^{\circ},w]
.

In cyclic preproofs we will annotate repeat nodes with the word
\mathrm{Rep}
at the right, instead of the rule at the node.

3
Wellfounded proof theory of bimodal provability

In this section, we define the wellfounded sequent calculi
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}
,
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CSM}
and
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{ER}
.
In addition, we show that
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}
corresponds to
\mathsf{CS}
,
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CSM}
to
\mathsf{CSM}
and
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{ER}
to
\mathsf{ER}
.

3.1
CS and CSM

A
sequent
is a pair
(\Gamma,\Delta)
of finite multisets of formulas, usually denoted by
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
.
\Gamma
is called the
left side
of the sequent while
\Delta
is called the
right side
.
Given a multisets of formulas
\Gamma
, we write
\Gamma^{s}
to mean the set obtained by deleting repetitions from
\Gamma
.
If
\Sigma
is a multiset
\{\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}\}
, then
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma
stands for
\{\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi_{1},\ldots,\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi_{n}\}
where
i\in\{0,1\}
. Moreover, we use
\overline{i}
for
1-i
, i.e.
\overline{0}=1
and vice versa.
Further,
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi
stands for
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi,\phi
and we use this similarly for multisets of formulas.

\mathrm{ax}

p,\Gamma\Rightarrow p,\Delta

{\bot}\mathrm{L}

\bot,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

{\bot}\mathrm{R}

\Gamma\Rightarrow\bot,\Delta

\Gamma\Rightarrow\phi,\Delta

\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

{\to}\mathrm{L}

\phi\to\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\phi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\psi,\Delta

{\to}\mathrm{R}

\Gamma\Rightarrow\phi\to\psi,\Delta

Figure 2:
Propositional rules

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\Rightarrow\phi

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CS}}_{i}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}},\Gamma\Rightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi,\Delta

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\Sigma_{0},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\Sigma_{1},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\Rightarrow\phi

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CSM}}_{0}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\Sigma_{0},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\Sigma_{1},\Gamma\Rightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi,\Delta

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\Sigma_{0},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\Sigma_{1},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\phi\Rightarrow\phi

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CSM}}_{1}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\Sigma_{0},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\Sigma_{1},\Gamma\Rightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi,\Delta

Figure 3:
Modal rules for
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}
and
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CSM}

The rules needed to define calculi for
\mathsf{CS}
and
\mathsf{CSM}
are given in Figures
2
and
3
.
In the rules
({\to}\mathrm{L})
,
({\to}\mathrm{R})
,
(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CS}}_{i})
and
(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CSM}}_{i})
the formula displayed at the conclusion is called the
principal formula
(of the rule instance) and the displayed
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits
-formula in the left side of the premise of the rules
(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CS}}_{i})
and
(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CSM}}_{i})
is called
diagonal formula
.
In
(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CS}}_{i})
and
(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CSM}}_{i})
the formulas belonging to
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\Sigma_{0},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\Sigma_{1}
are called
auxiliary formulas
.
In
(\mathrm{ax})
,
({\bot}\mathrm{L})
,
(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CS}}_{i})
and
(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CSM}}_{i})
the multisets
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are called the
weakening formulas
of the rule instance.
Notice that the weakening formulas of a rule instance can be changed arbitrarily and will still be a rule instance of the same rule.

In the propositional rules of Figure
2
, there are obvious omissions: namely, the usual rules
({\neg}\mathrm{L})
,
({\neg}\mathrm{R})
,
({\wedge}\mathrm{L})
,
({\wedge}\mathrm{R})
,
({\vee}\mathrm{L})
and
({\vee}\mathrm{R})
.
This omission is not problematic, as these rules can be simulated with the propositional rules of the system.
Let us show as an example how to simulate
({\neg}\mathrm{R})
and
({\vee}\mathrm{L})
, remembering that
\phi\vee\psi=\neg\phi\to\psi
.

\phi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

{\bot}\mathrm{R}

\phi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\bot,\Delta

{\to}\mathrm{R}

\Gamma\Rightarrow\neg\phi,\Delta

\phi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

{\neg}\mathrm{R}

\Gamma\Rightarrow\neg\phi,\Delta

\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

{\to}\mathrm{L}

\phi\vee\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

From now own we will use the rules
({\neg}\mathrm{L})
,
({\neg}\mathrm{R})
,
({\wedge}\mathrm{L})
,
({\wedge}\mathrm{R})
,
({\vee}\mathrm{L})
,
({\vee}\mathrm{R})
inside proofs as abbreviations for the corresponding application of rules.

{definition}

We define the following wellfounded sequent calculi:

1.

The calculus
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}
is defined as having the rules of Figure
2
together with the rules
(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CS}}_{0})
and
(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CS}}_{1})
from Figure
3
.

2.

The calculus
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CSM}
is defined as having the rules of Figure
2
together with the rules
(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CSM}}_{0})
and
(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CSM}}_{1})
from Figure
3
.

Then, it is easy to show the following lemma by induction on the complexity of
\phi
.

{lemma}

We have that
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}\vdash\phi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\phi,\Delta
and
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CSM}\vdash\phi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\phi,\Delta
.

When we use this lemma inside proofs in
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}
or
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CSM}
, we will annotate the sequent with the rule
(\mathrm{Ax})
.

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\mathrm{Wk}

\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}

\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}

\mathrm{Ctr}

\Gamma,(\Gamma^{\prime})^{s}\Rightarrow\Delta,(\Delta^{\prime})^{s}

where we remember that for a multiset
\Theta
,
\Theta^{s}
is
\Theta
without repeptitions.

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\chi

\chi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\mathrm{Cut}

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

Figure 4:
Structural rules

As usual, even if they do not form part of the sequent calculus explicitely, there are some structural rules (see Figure
4
) which we expect to behave well with the defined calculi.
In the following lemma we put together the structural properties of
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}
and
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CSM}
which are easy to show, all of them by induction on the height of the proof (using Theorem
2.2
when necessary).

{lemma}

In
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}
,
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CSM}
,
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}+\mathrm{Cut}
and
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CSM}+\mathrm{Cut}
we have the following.

1.

(\mathrm{Wk})
is eliminable and admissible preserving height and rules.

2.

({\bot}\mathrm{R})
,
({\to}\mathrm{L})
and
({\to}\mathrm{R})
are invertible preserving height and rules.

3.

(\mathrm{Ctr})
is eliminable and admissible preserving height and rules.

Showing cut elimination will require a bit more work and will be done in Section
4
.
However, using the previous lemma, we can establish the connection between
\mathsf{CS}
and
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}+\mathrm{Cut}
and between
\mathsf{CSM}
and
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}+\mathrm{Cut}
.
The first correspondence is in the following lemma.

{lemma}

For any formula
\phi
we have the following.

1.

\mathsf{CS}\vdash\phi
implies
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}+\mathrm{Cut}\vdash{\Rightarrow\phi}
.

2.

\mathsf{CSM}\vdash\phi
implies
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CSM}+\mathrm{Cut}\vdash{\Rightarrow\phi}
.

Proof.

Proof of 1.
It suffices to show that
\left\{\phi\mid\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}\vdash{\Rightarrow\phi}\right\}
contains the axioms of
\mathsf{CS}
and is closed under modus ponens,
(\mathrm{Nec}_{0})
and
(\mathrm{Nec}_{1})
.
That it contains all propositional tautologies is clear thanks to having rules
(\mathrm{ax})
,
({\bot}\mathrm{L})
,
({\to}\mathrm{L})
and
({\to}\mathrm{R})
.
The proofs of
(\mathrm{K}_{i})
,
(\mathrm{L}_{i})
and
(\mathrm{C}_{i,\overline{i}})
are as follows.

\mathrm{Ax}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\phi\to\psi),\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi,\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\psi\Rightarrow\phi,\psi

\mathrm{Ax}

\psi,\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\phi\to\psi),\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi,\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\psi\Rightarrow\psi

{\to}\mathrm{L}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\phi\to\psi),\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi,\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\psi\Rightarrow\psi

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CS}}_{i}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\phi\to\psi),\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\Rightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\psi

{\to}\mathrm{R}

\Rightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\phi\to\psi)\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\psi

\mathrm{Ax}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\phi),\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\Rightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi,\phi

\mathrm{Ax}

\phi,\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\phi),\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\Rightarrow\phi

{\to}\mathrm{L}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\phi),\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\Rightarrow\phi

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CS}}_{i}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\phi)\Rightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi

{\to}\mathrm{R}

\Rightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\phi)\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi

\mathrm{Ax}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi,\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\Rightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CS}}_{\overline{i}}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\Rightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi

{\to}\mathrm{R}

\Rightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi

All left to show are the closure properties. We start with Modus Ponens.
Assume
\pi\vdash{\Rightarrow\phi\to\psi}
and
\tau\vdash{\Rightarrow\phi}
in
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}+\mathrm{Cut}
, then we have the proof

\mathrm{Wk}(\tau)

\Rightarrow\psi,\phi

\mathrm{Inv}_{{\to}\mathrm{R}}(\pi)

\phi\Rightarrow\psi

\mathrm{Cut}

\Rightarrow\psi

Finally, for Necessitation, assume that
\pi\vdash{\Rightarrow\phi}
. Then the desired proof in
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}+\mathrm{Cut}
is

\mathrm{Wk}(\pi)

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\Rightarrow\phi

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CS}}_{i}

\Rightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi

Proof of 2.
The proof is largely the same. We just have to replace the rule
(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CS}}_{i})
in the previous proofs with
(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CSM}}_{i})
.
We only show that
(\mathrm{M}_{0,1})
is contained in
\left\{\phi\mid\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CSM}+\mathrm{Cut}\vdash{\Rightarrow\phi}\right\}
.

\mathrm{Ax}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi,\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\phi\Rightarrow\phi

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CSM}}_{i}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\Rightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\phi

{\to}\mathrm{R}

\Rightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\phi

∎

For the full correspondence, we need to interpret sequents as formulas.
Given a sequent
S=\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
we define its
formula interpretation
as

S^{\flat}=\bigwedge\Gamma\to\bigvee\Delta.

{definition}

Let
R
be a sequent rule and
L
be a logic.
We say that
R
is
sound in
L
if for any instance
(S_{0},\ldots,S_{n-1},S)\in R
we have that
L\vdash S^{\flat}_{i}
for all
i<n
implies
L\vdash S^{\flat}
.

{lemma}

The rules of
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}+\mathrm{Cut}
are sound in
\mathsf{CS}
and the rules of
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CSM}+\mathrm{Cut}
are sound in
\mathsf{CSM}
.

Proof.

Proof for
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}
.
First, let
R
be one of
(\mathrm{ax})
,
({\bot}\mathrm{L})
,
({\bot}\mathrm{R})
,
({\to}\mathrm{L})
,
({\to}\mathrm{R})
or
(\mathrm{Cut})
.
Then for any instance
(S_{0},\ldots,S_{n-1},S)\in R
we have that
\bigwedge_{i<n}S_{i}^{\flat}\to S^{\flat}
is a propositional tautology, and hence
\mathsf{CS}\vdash\bigwedge_{i<n}S_{i}^{\flat}\to S^{\flat}
. Soundness of the rule
R
follows trivially from this.

Finally, let
R=(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CS}}_{i})
and assume we have an instance

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\Rightarrow\phi

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CS}}_{i}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}},\Gamma\Rightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi,\Delta

Then we have the following reasoning

\displaystyle\mathsf{CS}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}}\wedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\phi,

\displaystyle\text{by assumption},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CS}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}}\to(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\phi),

\displaystyle\text{by propositional reasoning},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CS}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}}\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\phi),

\displaystyle\text{by ($\mathrm{Nec}_{i}$) and $(\mathrm{K}_{i})$},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CS}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}}\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\phi),

\displaystyle\text{by transitivity},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CS}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}}\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\phi),

\displaystyle\text{by $(\mathrm{C}_{i,\overline{i}})$},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CS}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}}\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi,

\displaystyle\text{by $(\mathrm{L}_{i})$},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CS}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}}\wedge\bigwedge\Gamma\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\vee\bigvee\Delta,

\displaystyle\text{by propositional reasoning},

as desired.

Proof for
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CSM}
.
In case
R
is one of
(\mathrm{ax})
,
({\bot}\mathrm{L})
,
({\bot}\mathrm{R})
,
({\to}\mathrm{L})
,
({\to}\mathrm{R})
,
(\mathrm{Cut})
or
(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CSM}}_{0})
the reasoning is the same as for
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}
.
So assume
R=(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CSM}}_{1})
and that we have an instance

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\Sigma_{0},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\Sigma,\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\phi\Rightarrow\phi

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{CSM}}_{1}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\Sigma_{0},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\Sigma,\Gamma\Rightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\phi,\Delta

Then we have the following reasoning

\displaystyle\mathsf{CSM}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\Sigma_{0}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\Sigma_{1}\wedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\phi\to\phi,

\displaystyle\text{by assumption},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CSM}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\Sigma_{0}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\Sigma_{1}\to(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\phi\to\phi),

\displaystyle\text{by propositional reasoning},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CSM}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\Sigma_{0}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\Sigma_{1}\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\phi\to\phi),

\displaystyle\text{by ($\mathrm{Nec}_{1}$) and $(\mathrm{K}_{1})$},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CSM}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\Sigma_{0}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\Sigma_{1}\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\phi\to\phi),

\displaystyle\text{by transitivity},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CSM}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\Sigma_{0}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\Sigma_{0}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\Sigma_{1}\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\phi\to\phi),

\displaystyle\text{by $(\mathrm{C}_{0,1})$},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CSM}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\Sigma_{0}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\Sigma_{1}\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\phi\to\phi),

\displaystyle\text{by $(\mathrm{M}_{0,1})$},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CSM}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\Sigma_{0}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\Sigma_{1}\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\phi,

\displaystyle\text{by $(\mathrm{L}_{i})$},

\displaystyle\mathsf{CSM}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\Sigma_{0}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\Sigma_{1}\wedge\bigwedge\Gamma\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\phi\vee\bigvee\Delta,

\displaystyle\text{by propositional reasoning},

as desired.
∎

The full correspondence is stated in the following theorem.

3.1
.

Let
S
be a sequent.
We have that:

1.

\mathsf{CS}\vdash S^{\flat}
iff
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}+\mathrm{Cut}\vdash S
.

2.

\mathsf{CSM}\vdash S^{\flat}
iff
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CSM}+\mathrm{Cut}\vdash S
.

Proof.

Proof of 1.
Let
S=\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
.
First assume that
\mathsf{CS}\vdash\bigwedge\Gamma\to\bigvee\Delta
, then by Lemma
3.1
using invertibility of propositional rules we get that
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}+\mathrm{Cut}\vdash{\Rightarrow\bigwedge\Gamma\to\bigvee\Delta}
.
By invertibility of the propositional rules we obtain the desired
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}+\mathrm{Cut}\vdash\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
.
For the opposite direction it suffices to use Lemma
3.1
with an induction on the height of the sequent proof.

Proof of 2. Analogous to the proof of 1.
∎

3.2
ER

In order to acommodate the axiom
(\mathrm{ER}_{1,0})
we need to make
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}
reflexive, but only when it is directly under a
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}
modality.
Hence we need a mechanism to control when we are directly under a
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}
modality in a proof and allow the use of the reflexivity rule only in those situations.
For that purpose we will define a calculus with two different kinds of sequents, as shown below.

An
\mathsf{ER}
-
sequent
is a triple
(\Gamma,\Delta,i)
where
\Gamma,\Delta
are multisets of formulas and
i\in\left\{0,1\right\}
.
We will denote
(\Gamma,\Delta,0)
by
\Gamma\Rightarrow_{0}\Delta
and
(\Gamma,\Delta,1)
by
\Gamma\Rightarrow_{1}\Delta
.
\Gamma
is called the
left side
of the sequent while
\Delta
is called the
right side
.
We will use
\gg
to mean either
\Rightarrow_{0}
or
\Rightarrow_{1}
(or also
\Rightarrow
if we want to talk about non-
\mathsf{ER}
-sequents and
\mathsf{ER}
-sequents at the same time).

\mathrm{ax}

p,\Gamma\Rightarrow_{i}p,\Delta

{\bot}\mathrm{L}

\bot,\Gamma\Rightarrow_{i}\Delta

\Gamma\Rightarrow_{i}\Delta

{\bot}\mathrm{R}

\Gamma\Rightarrow_{i}\bot,\Delta

\Gamma\Rightarrow_{i}\phi,\Delta

\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow_{i}\Delta

{\to}\mathrm{L}

\phi\to\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow_{i}\Delta

\phi,\Gamma\Rightarrow_{i}\psi,\Delta

{\to}\mathrm{R}

\Gamma\Rightarrow_{i}\phi\to\psi,\Delta

Figure 5:
Propositional rules for
\mathsf{ER}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\Rightarrow_{i}\phi

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{ER}}_{i}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}},\Gamma\gg\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi,\Delta

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi,\Gamma\Rightarrow_{1}\Delta

\mathrm{ER}_{1,0}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi,\Gamma\Rightarrow_{1}\Delta

Figure 6:
Modal rules for
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{ER}

The rules that we are going to need to define calculi for
\mathsf{ER}
can be found at Figures
5
and
6
.
In the rules
({\to}\mathrm{L})
,
({\to}\mathrm{R})

{lemma}

.

Proof.

The only new axiom is

{\to}\mathrm{R}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\to\phi)\Rightarrow_{1}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\to\phi

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{ER}}_{1}

\Rightarrow_{0}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\to\phi)

.
∎

As before, for the full correspondence we need to interpret
\mathsf{ER}
-sequents as formulas.
Given a sequent

we define its

as

S^{\flat}=\begin{cases}\bigwedge\Gamma\to\bigvee\Delta&\text{if $i=0$},\\
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}(\bigwedge\Gamma\to\bigvee\Delta)&\text{if $i=1$}.\end{cases}

{lemma}

The rules of
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{ER}+\mathrm{Cut}
are sound in
\mathsf{ER}
.

Proof.

If
R
is one of
(\mathrm{ax})
,
({\bot}\mathrm{L})
,
({\bot}\mathrm{R})
,
({\to}\mathrm{L})
,
({\to}\mathrm{R})
or
(\mathrm{Cut})
with
\Rightarrow_{0}
-sequents, then the proof is exactly as in Lemma
3.1
.
Assume
R
is one of those rules but with
\Rightarrow_{1}
-sequents and consider an instance

\Gamma_{0}\Rightarrow_{1}\Delta_{0}

\cdots

\Gamma_{n-1}\Rightarrow_{1}\Delta_{n-1}

R

\Gamma\Rightarrow_{1}\Delta

Note that
\mathsf{ER}\vdash(\bigwedge_{i<n}\bigwedge\Gamma_{i}\to\bigvee\Delta_{i})\to(\bigwedge\Gamma\to\bigvee\Delta)
as it is a propositional tautology.
Then using
(\mathrm{Nec}_{1})
and
(\mathrm{K}_{1})
we obtain that
\mathsf{ER}\vdash(\bigwedge_{i<n}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}(\bigwedge\Gamma_{i}\to\bigvee\Delta_{i}))\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}(\bigwedge\Gamma\to\bigvee\Delta)
, from where the desired soundness follows.

Assume
R
is
(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{ER}}_{i})
and that we have an instance

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\Rightarrow_{0}\phi

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{ER}}_{i}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}},\Gamma\gg\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi,\Delta

Then we have the following reasoning

\displaystyle\mathsf{ER}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}}\wedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\phi,

\displaystyle\text{by assumption},

\displaystyle\mathsf{ER}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}}\to(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\phi),

\displaystyle\text{by propositional reasoning},

\displaystyle\mathsf{ER}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}}\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\phi),

\displaystyle\text{by $(\mathrm{Nec}_{i})$ and $(\mathrm{K}_{i})$},

\displaystyle\mathsf{ER}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}}\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\phi),

\displaystyle\text{by $(4_{i})$},

\displaystyle\mathsf{ER}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}}\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\to\phi),

\displaystyle\text{by $(\mathrm{C}_{i,\overline{i}})$},

\displaystyle\mathsf{ER}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}}\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi,

\displaystyle\text{by $(\mathrm{L}_{i})$},

\displaystyle\mathsf{ER}\vdash\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}}\wedge\bigwedge\Gamma\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\vee\bigvee\Delta,

\displaystyle\text{by propositional reasoning},

\displaystyle\mathsf{ER}\vdash\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\left(\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i}\wedge\bigwedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}}\wedge\bigwedge\Gamma\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi\vee\bigvee\Delta\right),

\displaystyle\text{by $(\mathrm{Nec}_{1})$ (only if ${\gg}\text{ is }{\Rightarrow_{1}}$)}.

Assume
R
is
(\mathrm{ER}_{1,0})
, that we have an instance

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi,\Gamma\Rightarrow_{1}\Delta

\mathrm{ER}_{1,0}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi,\Gamma\Rightarrow_{1}\Delta

and that
\mathsf{ER}\vdash\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}(\phi\wedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\wedge\bigwedge\Gamma\to\bigvee\Delta)
.
By propositional reasoning

\mathsf{ER}\vdash(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\to\phi)\wedge(\phi\wedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\wedge\bigwedge\Gamma\to\bigvee\Delta)\to(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\wedge\bigwedge\Gamma\to\bigvee\Delta),

so using
(\mathrm{Nec}_{1})
and
(\mathrm{K}_{1})
we obtain

\mathsf{ER}\vdash\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\to\phi)\wedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}(\phi\wedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\wedge\bigwedge\Gamma\to\bigvee\Delta)\to\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\wedge\bigwedge\Gamma\to\bigvee\Delta).

Since
\mathsf{ER}\vdash\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\to\phi)
by axiom
(\mathrm{ER}_{1,0})
and
\mathsf{ER}\vdash\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}(\phi\wedge\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\wedge\bigwedge\Gamma\to\bigvee\Delta)
we can conclude the desired
\mathsf{ER}\vdash\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}(\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\phi\wedge\bigwedge\Gamma\to\bigvee\Delta)
.
∎

Notice that the correspondence shown in the next theorem is only partial.
In particular, it only works for sequents with the
\Rightarrow_{0}
arrow.
For the full correspondence, i.e. for all sequents, we will first need to prove cut elimination.

3.2
.

Let
S
be a
\Rightarrow_{0}
-sequent.
We have that
\mathsf{ER}\vdash S^{\flat}
iff
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{ER}+\mathrm{Cut}\vdash S
.

Proof.

The proof is completely analogous to Theorem
3.1
but using Lemmas
3.2
and
3.2
instead of Lemmas
3.1
and
3.1
.
∎

4
Cut elimination

The methodology that we will use to show cut elimination for
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CS}
,
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{CSM}
and
\mathcal{G}\mathsf{ER}
will be local progress proof theory.
The advantage of this approach is that the calculi do not have diagonal formulas, which greatly simplifies the inductive measure needed in the cut elimination procedure.
In particular, contrary to the original proof
[
44
]
, this cut elimination does not need to make a deep analysis (i.e., follow the trace of a formula upwards) of the proofs above the cut rule; instead it suffices to make a case distinction on their last rules.
3
3

3

Another simplified cut elimination for
\mathsf{GL}
was obtained in
[
11
,
20
]
, using a measure based on proof search. We prefer to show cut elimination in a non-wellfounded system as this can also be used to establish uniform Lyndon interpolation.

If wanted, this cut-elimination procedure could be made effective as it is only necessary to approximate a big enough portion of the non-wellfounded proof.
However, since we do not need the effectiveness of cut elimination for our other results, we will keep the exposition as simple as possible and disregard the effectiveness.

4.1
Non-wellfounded calculi

The connection of provability logics with non-wellfounded calculi is wellknown since the work of Savateev and Shamkanov
[
35
]
.
As usual, non-wellfounded calculi for the logics
\mathsf{CS}
,
\mathsf{CSM}
and
\mathsf{ER}
can be obtained from the wellfounded calculi by removing the diagonal formula in the modal rules.

The particular rules needed are shown in Figure
8
.
Again, the formula displayed in the conclusion is called the
principal formula
(of the rule instance) and the formulas in
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{0}\Sigma_{0}
,
\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{1}\Sigma_{1}
will be called
auxiliary formulas
.

\Gamma
and
\Delta
will be called the
weakening formulas
of the rule instance, and it still holds that arbitarily changing the weakening formulas of a rule instance still makes it a rule instance of the same rule.

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquaredot$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}}\Rightarrow\phi

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits^{\mathsf{K4CS}}_{i}

\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\Sigma_{i},\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{\overline{i}}\Sigma_{\overline{i}},\Gamma\Rightarrow\mathop{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\scalebox{1.3}{$\medsquare$}}}}\nolimits_{i}\phi,\Delta
